Meaning of “same mathematical argument”. The hypotheses, intermediate mathematical claims, and logical implications agree after unfolding definitions. This does not mean literal equality of prose and tactic scripts or identical order of independent estimates. A local “same” tag does not erase a different argument in one of its dependencies. The correspondence annotations are reviewed mathematical judgments; Lean checks the formal proofs, not their linguistic identity to the manuscript.

Findings

Named mathematical results completed

All 21 labelled lemmas, propositions, theorems, corollaries, and remarks now have formal counterparts. The former gaps — the one-sided quadratic example, the uncapped-exception counterexample, and the additional residual-to-distance estimate — are closed. This is a coverage statement for the supplied manuscript snapshot, not for every sentence or the complete C# implementation.

Two κ arguments, two complete routes

The main scaled-error theorem now follows the derivative/mean-value proof, including κ′ and its bounds. The later machine-formalization theorem retains its algebraic factorization. Both routes reach the concrete binary64 word error theorem. The 10ε product estimate now precedes the 8ε estimate, matching the displayed manuscript order.

Examples connected to actual AB steps

The example recurrences are derived from the original secants and retained-ordinate correction. Every state is linked by induction to an exact AB trajectory; interiority, true residual signs, nonzero values, and positive retained ordinates are proved. The counterexample includes both partial derivatives, the invariant rectangle, the 0.49 residual ratio, indefinite uncapped continuation, width limit one, the iteration-14 bound violation, and stopping-test failures.

Executable bridges completed

Domain is now explicitly equivalent to opposite nonzero endpoint signs. The original coefficient equals κ(ρ) for both sign orientations. Three-input wrappers end at the original inequality, and the 57ε guarantee ends at executable comparisons.

Arithmetic scopes

Global convergence is an exact-real-arithmetic theorem for any interior proposals obeying the sign update and capped width policy. The unscaled estimate concerns ten concrete nearest-even operations with normal-or-zero exact intermediates and no overflow; its final difference is exact. The existing scaled theorem reaches IEEE words and the translated C# arithmetic tree.

Residual-to-distance estimate

The mean-value point and derivative lower bound imply |x−z|≤|f(x)|/m. The formal result needs a selected zero, not its uniqueness; continuity and interior differentiability are explicit. It is therefore stronger than the manuscript statement in that respect.

Implementation boundary

Function-evaluation error, the invalid-input branch, compiler/JIT/hardware, and binary64 convergence of the whole root-finding loop remain outside the proved scope. The unscaled expression has a concrete nearest-even analysis under its stated domain, but no additional word-level/C# translation theorem.

A cubic bound for the switching expression

Same mathematical argument

lem:switch_cubic · manuscript-main.tex:306–346

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Cubic.Q, ModAB.Cubic.Q_expanded, ModAB.Cubic.Q_hasDerivAt, ModAB.Cubic.Q_derivative_negative, ModAB.Cubic.Q_strictAntiOn, ModAB.Cubic.Q_bounds, ModAB.Cubic.endpoint_difference, ModAB.Cubic.endpoint_difference_pos, ModAB.Cubic.midpoint_bound, ModAB.Cubic.ratio_range, ModAB.Cubic.switching_bound

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[A cubic bound for the switching expression]
\label{lem:switch_cubic}
Let $f_1$ and $f_2$ be nonzero real endpoint values of opposite signs, and put
\begin{equation}
 D=|f_2-f_1|=|f_1|+|f_2|,\qquad
 y_m=\frac{f_1+f_2}{2},\qquad
 v=\frac{|y_m|}{D},\qquad k=(1-v)^2.
 \label{eq:cubic_definitions}
\end{equation}
For any real midpoint residual $y_3$, let $H=\max(D,|y_3|)$.
Then $0\le v\le1/2$ and, in exact arithmetic,
\begin{equation}
 k|y_m|+k|y_3|\le H(1-v)^2(1+v)\le H.
 \label{eq:cubic_switch_bound}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The triangle inequality gives
\[
 |y_m|=\tfrac12|f_1+f_2|
 \le\tfrac12(|f_1|+|f_2|)=D/2.
\]
Since $D>0$, division by $D$ gives $0\le v\le1/2$. Moreover,
\[
 k|y_m|+k|y_3|
 =(1-v)^2(vD+|y_3|)
 \le H(1-v)^2(1+v).
\]
To bound the remaining dimensionless factor, define
\[
 Q(v)=(1-v)^2(1+v)=1-v-v^2+v^3.
\]
Its derivative satisfies
\[
 Q'(v)=3v^2-2v-1=(v-1)(3v+1)<0
 \qquad (0\le v\le1/2).
\]
Thus $Q$ is decreasing, and its endpoint values yield
$3/8=Q(1/2)\le Q(v)\le Q(0)=1$.
Substitution proves \eqref{eq:cubic_switch_bound}.
\end{proof}

Interiority of the AB approximation

Same mathematical argument

lem:interiority · manuscript-main.tex:454–476

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.secant_convex, ModAB.Convergence.secant_interior, ModAB.Convergence.proposal_interior

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Interiority of the AB approximation]
\label{lem:interiority}
Let $a<b$ and $uv<0$.  Then
\[
    x=\frac{av-bu}{v-u}
\]
belongs to $(a,b)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume without loss of generality that $u<0<v$.  Then
\[
    x
    =
    \frac{av+b|u|}{v+|u|}
    =
    \lambda a+(1-\lambda)b,
    \qquad
    \lambda=\frac{v}{v+|u|}\in(0,1).
\]
Thus $x$ is a strict convex combination of $a$ and $b$ and belongs to
$(a,b)$. The case $v<0<u$ follows in the same way.
\end{proof}

Preservation of the signs of the modified ordinates

Same mathematical argument

lem:working_signs · manuscript-main.tex:478–490

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.SameSign, ModAB.Convergence.positive_correction, ModAB.Convergence.inserted_sign, ModAB.Convergence.working_opposite, ModAB.Convergence.proposal_interior

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Preservation of the signs of the modified ordinates]
\label{lem:working_signs}
Suppose \eqref{eq:working_signs} holds.  If the retained modified ordinate is
multiplied by a finite factor $\theta>0$ and the newly inserted endpoint
receives the actual function value at the new point, then
\eqref{eq:working_signs} holds at the next nonterminating AB step.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Multiplication by a positive factor preserves the sign of the retained
ordinate. At the new endpoint, the modified ordinate equals the actual value of the function. Since the new interval has endpoint values of opposite
signs, the modified ordinates also have opposite signs.
\end{proof}

Nested intervals containing a zero

Same mathematical argument

prop:bracket · manuscript-main.tex:492–522

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.sign_update, ModAB.Convergence.interval_update, ModAB.Convergence.bisection_width, ModAB.Convergence.bracket_contains_zero, ModAB.Convergence.Updated, ModAB.Convergence.updated_invariants, ModAB.Convergence.exact_invariants, ModAB.Convergence.exact_zeros, ModAB.Convergence.active_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.active_nonincrease

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{proposition}[Nested intervals containing a zero]
\label{prop:bracket}
At every nonterminating iteration,
\begin{equation}
    f(a_n)f(b_n)<0,
    \qquad
    I_{n+1}\subseteq I_n.
    \label{eq:nested_brackets}
\end{equation}
Consequently, every $I_n$ contains at least one zero of $f$, and the widths
\begin{equation}
    W_n=b_n-a_n
    \label{eq:width}
\end{equation}
are nonincreasing.  A bisection step gives
\begin{equation}
    W_{n+1}=\frac{1}{2}W_n.
    \label{eq:bisection_halving}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The sign condition holds initially by Assumption~1. The midpoint belongs
to the current interval, as does the AB approximation by
Lemma~\ref{lem:interiority}. Unless this new point is an exact zero,
precisely one of the two resulting subintervals has endpoint values of
opposite signs. The method selects this subinterval, which contains a
zero by continuity and the intermediate value theorem. This proves the
nesting property and the monotonicity of the interval lengths. A
bisection step gives a subinterval of half the preceding length.
\end{proof}

Decrease of the smaller endpoint residual

Same mathematical argument

lem:residual_progress · manuscript-main.tex:526–544

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.residual_progress, ModAB.Convergence.residual_progress_iterated

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Decrease of the smaller endpoint residual]
\label{lem:residual_progress}
Let $R^-=\min(|f(a)|,|f(b)|)>0$ before a nonterminating step within
the interval, and let
$R^+$ be the corresponding minimum after the sign-preserving update.
If the new residual satisfies $|f(x)|<\eta R^-$ with $0<\eta<1$, then
\begin{equation}
 R^+=|f(x)|<\eta R^-.
 \label{eq:residual_contraction}
\end{equation}
For $k$ consecutive such steps, the final minimum is less than
$\eta^k$ times its initial value.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The retained endpoint has residual magnitude at least $R^-$. The newly
inserted endpoint has residual magnitude below $\eta R^-<R^-$. It therefore
becomes the endpoint with the smaller residual magnitude, which proves
\eqref{eq:residual_contraction}. Repeated substitution proves the last claim.
\end{proof}

A one-sided quadratic AB phase

prop:one_sided · manuscript-main.tex:562–595

Corresponding Lean declarations

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{proposition}[A one-sided quadratic AB phase]
\label{prop:one_sided}
For the continuously differentiable function
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=\begin{cases}x,&x\le0,\\x+x^2,&x>0,\end{cases}
 \label{eq:one_sided_function}
\end{equation}
an AB phase starting from $[-1,1]$ with the actual endpoint values as its
initial ordinates has a fixed
right endpoint. If interpolation is continued without a width fallback,
the left-endpoint error converges quadratically to zero, whereas the bracket
width tends to one.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write the left endpoint as $-e$ and the right modified ordinate as $v>1$.
The actual left ordinate is $-e$. The next secant approximation is $-e'$, where
\[
 e'=\frac{e(v-1)}{v+e}>0.
\]
Starting with $e=1,v=2$, the first two errors are $1/3$ and $1/7$.
\rev{The second consecutive update of the left endpoint activates the AB correction.} Whenever it is applied,
\[
 v'=v\left(1-\frac{e'}e\right)
    =\frac{v(1+e)}{v+e}=1+e'.
\]
Thus the next trial point remains to the left and, from the error $1/7$ onward,
\begin{equation}
 e_{k+1}=\frac{e_k^2}{1+2e_k}.
 \label{eq:one_sided_quadratic}
\end{equation}
This positive sequence decreases to zero and
$e_{k+1}/e_k^2\to1$. Its first subsequent values are $1/63$, $1/4095$ and
$1/16777215$. The right endpoint stays at one, so $W_k=1+e_k\to1$.
\end{proof}

ModAB.Examples.counter_full_actual_update, ModAB.Examples.uncappedContinues, ModAB.Examples.counter_uncapped_continues, ModAB.Examples.counter_no_width_termination, ModAB.Examples.counter_mixed_tolerance_fails, ModAB.Examples.counter_global_bound_violation, ModAB.Examples.uncapped_counterexample

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{proposition}[Residual contraction without width convergence]
\label{prop:uncapped_counterexample}
Consider the usual AB correction, the switching test
\eqref{eq:switch_test}, and the rule with $B=2$, $\eta=1/2$, but with
no bound on the number of residual exceptions. For
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=\begin{cases}-x^2,&x<0,\\7x^2,&x\ge0,\end{cases}
 \qquad [a_0,b_0]=[-3,1],
 \label{eq:cap_counterexample}
\end{equation}
the exact-arithmetic process enters an AB phase after its first bisection
and never leaves it. The left endpoints converge to the unique
zero, while the right endpoint remains $1$ and the width tends to $1$.
From the third AB step onward, the ratio of the new residual magnitude to
the smaller preceding endpoint residual magnitude is at most $0.49<1/2$.
The function belongs to $C^1(\mathbb R)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The only zero is $0$. On the two open half-lines the derivatives are $-2x$
and $14x$, respectively. At zero,
$f'(0)=\lim_{h\to0}f(h)/h=0$, and both one-sided derivatives tend to zero.
This proves the stated regularity.

Initially $f(-3)=-9$ and $f(1)=7$. The first midpoint is $-1$, with
$f(-1)=-1$, and the chord midpoint value is also $(-9+7)/2=-1$.
Consequently, the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:switch_test} is zero and
its right-hand side is positive. The method enters AB on $[-1,1]$.

Let $j$ count AB steps after this entry. Write the left endpoint as $-e_j$
and the retained right modified ordinate as $v_j$, with $e_0=1$ and $v_0=7$.
The actual function value at the left endpoint is $-e_j^2$. If the next trial point remains to the
left of the zero, the secant formula gives
\begin{equation}
 e_j=\frac{e_{j-1}v_{j-1}-e_{j-1}^2}
              {v_{j-1}+e_{j-1}^2}.
 \label{eq:cap_secant_error}
\end{equation}
There is no retained-ordinate rescaling on the first AB step. Hence
\[
 e_1=\frac{7-1}{7+1}=\frac34,\qquad v_1=7,
 \qquad e_2=\frac{7e_1-e_1^2}{7+e_1^2}=\frac{75}{121}.
\]
Both approximations lie strictly to the left of zero and are closer to
it than the preceding left endpoint.

For two successive updates of that endpoint, the AB multiplier is
$1-e_j^2/e_{j-1}^2$, which is positive whenever $0<e_j<e_{j-1}$.
Rearranging \eqref{eq:cap_secant_error} gives
\[
 (e_{j-1}-e_j)v_{j-1}=e_{j-1}^2(1+e_j).
\]
Thus, for $j\ge2$ while these inequalities hold,
\begin{align}
 v_j
 &=v_{j-1}\left(1-\frac{e_j^2}{e_{j-1}^2}\right)\notag\\
 &=\frac{e_{j-1}^2(1+e_j)}{e_{j-1}-e_j}
   \frac{(e_{j-1}-e_j)(e_{j-1}+e_j)}{e_{j-1}^2}\notag\\
 &=(1+e_j)(e_{j-1}+e_j).
 \label{eq:cap_working_ordinate}
\end{align}
Applying this identity at $j=2$ and then taking the next secant yields
\[
 e_3=\frac{146325}{384296},\qquad
 \rho_3:=\frac{e_3}{e_2}=\frac{1951}{3176}.
\]
In particular, $0<e_3<2/5$ and $1/2<\rho_3<7/10$.

To prove that all subsequent approximations have the same properties, put $\rho_j=e_j/e_{j-1}$.
Using \eqref{eq:cap_working_ordinate} in the next secant, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \frac{e_{j+1}}{e_j}
 &=\frac{v_j-e_j}{v_j+e_j^2}\\
 &=\frac{(1+e_j)e_{j-1}+e_j^2}
         {(1+e_j)e_{j-1}+e_j+2e_j^2}\\
 &=\frac{1+e_j(1+\rho_j)}
         {1+\rho_j+e_j(1+2\rho_j)}.
\end{align*}
The last equality divides numerator and denominator by $e_{j-1}>0$.
Define
\[
 \Phi(e,\rho)=\frac{1+e(1+\rho)}{1+\rho+e(1+2\rho)}.
\]
For $e\ge0$ and $\rho>0$ its denominator is positive, and direct
differentiation gives
\[
 \frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\rho}
 =-\frac{(1+e)^2}{[1+\rho+e(1+2\rho)]^2}<0,\qquad
 \frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial e}
 =\frac{\rho^2}{[1+\rho+e(1+2\rho)]^2}>0.
\]
On $0\le e\le2/5$, $1/2\le\rho\le7/10$, monotonicity therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{10}{17}=\Phi(0,7/10)
 \le\Phi(e,\rho)\le\Phi(2/5,1/2)=\frac{16}{23}<\frac7{10}.
 \label{eq:cap_invariant_region}
\end{equation}
Both bounds belong to $[1/2,7/10]$. Since the required inequalities hold
for $j=3$, induction gives
\[
 0<e_{j+1}\le\tfrac7{10}e_j,\qquad
 \rho_{j+1}\in[1/2,7/10].
\]
These inequalities simultaneously prove that the next trial point remains
strictly left of zero, that the AB multiplier stays positive, and that
the hypotheses are preserved. Thus $e_j\to0$ and the right endpoint
is never replaced.

The actual value of the function at the right endpoint is $7$ and the left residual magnitude is
$e_j^2\le1$. Before AB step $j$ the minimum residual magnitude is therefore
$e_{j-1}^2$. For every $j\ge3$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{|f(-e_j)|}{\min\{|f(-e_{j-1})|,|f(1)|\}}
 =\rho_j^2\le(7/10)^2=0.49<\tfrac12.
 \label{eq:cap_residual_ratio}
\end{equation}

At the beginning of the phase the width is $S=2$, so the threshold at AB step $j$ is
$T_j=4/2^{j-1}$. The first two interval lengths satisfy the respective bounds
$4$ and $2$. For $j\ge3$, however, $T_j\le1$ while
$W_j^{\rm AB}=1+e_j>1$. The width condition is therefore violated at every subsequent step,
whereas \eqref{eq:cap_residual_ratio} permits a residual exception at each
of these steps. Without a bound on their number, the AB phase continues
indefinitely and the interval length tends to $1$.
\end{proof}

Geometric upper bound for an AB phase

Same mathematical argument

lem:adaptive_envelope · manuscript-main.tex:763–787

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.adaptiveMode, ModAB.Convergence.adaptive_step, ModAB.Convergence.threshold, ModAB.Convergence.envelopeConstant, ModAB.Convergence.ActivePhase, ModAB.Convergence.active_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.active_nonincrease, ModAB.Convergence.last_width_success, ModAB.Convergence.threshold_shift, ModAB.Convergence.active_envelope, ModAB.Convergence.C_at_least_one

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Geometric upper bound for an AB phase]
\label{lem:adaptive_envelope}
Let a phase start with width $S$, use \eqref{eq:adaptive_threshold}, and permit
at most $M$ consecutive width-test exceptions while active. If it is
still active after $t$ steps, then
\begin{equation}
 W_t\le\frac{CS}{2^t},\qquad C=2^{M+1}B.
 \label{eq:fallback_threshold}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If the width condition is satisfied at step $t$, then $W_t\le T_t$,
which is stronger than \eqref{eq:fallback_threshold}. Otherwise, let
$j=t-c$ be the last index at which it was satisfied. Since the phase
continues, $c\le M$. If the condition has not yet been satisfied, take
$j=0$ and set $T_0=2BS$; then $W_0=S\le T_0$ because $B\ge1/2$.
The bracket widths are nonincreasing, so in either case
\[
 W_t\le W_j\le T_j=2^cT_t
       \le 2^M\frac{2BS}{2^t}=\frac{CS}{2^t}.
\]
Whenever the width condition is satisfied, the counter returns to zero
and the estimate $W_j\le T_j$ holds at that index. The same argument
therefore applies after any number of such resets.
\end{proof}

Global convergence

Same mathematical argument

thm:global_simple · manuscript-main.tex:827–874

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.interval_distance, ModAB.Convergence.chosen_points_tendsto, ModAB.Convergence.nested_intervals_root, ModAB.Convergence.ExactRun, ModAB.Convergence.ExactRun.widthRun, ModAB.Convergence.global_convergence, ModAB.Convergence.Step, ModAB.Convergence.Run, ModAB.Convergence.step_history, ModAB.Convergence.step_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.history_of_run, ModAB.Convergence.run_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.isBisection, ModAB.Convergence.bisectionCount, ModAB.Convergence.count_monotone, ModAB.Convergence.step_count_bound, ModAB.Convergence.bisection_count_bound, ModAB.Convergence.count_unbounded, ModAB.Convergence.dyadic_tendsto, ModAB.Convergence.infinite_bisections, ModAB.Convergence.active_step, ModAB.Convergence.active_history, ModAB.Convergence.continuing_suffix, ModAB.Convergence.finite_bisections, ModAB.Convergence.width_tendsto_zero, ModAB.Convergence.width_tendsto_zero_quantitative

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{theorem}[Global convergence]
\label{thm:global_simple}
Under Assumption~1, with the actual function values and modified ordinates
kept separate and the adaptive rule of Section~\ref{sec:method}, the
exact-arithmetic iteration, continued without an iteration limit, either
terminates at an exact zero or satisfies
\[
    W_n\longrightarrow0.
\]
In the latter case, the nested brackets shrink to a single point $x^\ast$, and that point satisfies
$f(x^\ast)=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We distinguish two cases.

\emph{Case 1: infinitely many bisection steps.}
Each bisection halves the current interval length, and no intervening AB
step increases it. After $j$ bisections the length is therefore at most
$W_0/2^j$, which tends to zero.

\emph{Case 2: finitely many bisection steps.}
After the last bisection, all subsequent steps belong to a single AB
phase. Indeed, ending that phase would require another bisection, contrary
to the choice of the last one.

Let $W_c$ be the width at the start of that phase and let $W(t)$ be the width
after $t$ AB steps.  Because the phase is still active, Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive_envelope} gives
\[
    W(t)\leq \frac{C W_c}{2^t}.
\]
The right-hand side tends to zero, and hence so does $W(t)$.

Thus $W_n\to0$ in either case.  By
Proposition~\ref{prop:bracket}, the closed intervals $I_n$ are nonempty,
nested, and contain a zero.  A standard nested-interval argument now shows that
their intersection consists of one point:
\[
    \bigcap_{n=0}^{\infty} I_n=\{x^\ast\}.
\]
To verify that this point is a zero, choose any zero $z_n\in I_n$.  Both
$z_n$ and $x^\ast$ lie in the same interval, so
\[
    |z_n-x^\ast|\leq W_n\longrightarrow0.
\]
Thus $z_n\to x^\ast$.  Since $f(z_n)=0$ and $f$ is continuous,
$f(x^\ast)=0$.
\end{proof}

Scope of the convergence argument

Same mathematical argument

rem:proof_independence · manuscript-main.tex:876–884

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.adaptiveMode, ModAB.Convergence.adaptive_step, ModAB.Convergence.ExactRun, ModAB.Convergence.Step, ModAB.Convergence.Run, ModAB.Convergence.ActivePhase, ModAB.Convergence.Mode, ModAB.Convergence.History

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{remark}[Scope of the convergence argument]
\label{rem:proof_independence}
The argument does not assume differentiability, a unique zero, a particular
local convergence order, or the specific switching test
\eqref{eq:switch_test}.  It uses only intervals whose endpoint values have opposite signs,
interpolation points within these intervals, nonincreasing interval
lengths, the geometric upper bound during each continuing AB phase, and
the bisection step that follows the end of a phase.
\end{remark}

Guaranteed shrinkage of a completed AB phase

Same mathematical argument

lem:completed_phase · manuscript-main.tex:911–960

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.envelope_to_dyadic, ModAB.Convergence.phaseCredit, ModAB.Convergence.completed_phase

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Guaranteed shrinkage of a completed AB phase]
\label{lem:completed_phase}
Let a completed phase block start with width $S$.  At its end the width is at
most $S/2^h$, where
\begin{equation}
    h=
    \begin{cases}
       1, & t\leq d+1,\\
       t-d, & t\geq d+2.
    \end{cases}
    \label{eq:phase_credit}
\end{equation}
In both cases,
\begin{equation}
    \ell\leq qh.
    \label{eq:length_credit}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We consider the two ranges of $t$ separately.

If $t\leq d+1$, the AB steps do not increase the interval length, and the
following bisection halves it. Thus $h=1$, and
\[
    \ell=t+1\leq d+2=q=qh.
\]

Now suppose that $t\geq d+2$.  The phase was still active after step $t-1$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive_envelope} therefore gives
\[
    W(t-1)
    \leq
    \frac{CS}{2^{t-1}}
    \leq
    \frac{S}{2^{t-d-1}},
\]
where the last inequality uses $C\leq2^d$.  The $t$th AB step cannot increase
the width, and the following bisection divides it by two.  Hence
\[
    W_{\mathrm{end}}
    \leq
    \frac{S}{2^{t-d}}.
\]
The block therefore guarantees $h=t-d$ halvings.  Since $h\geq2$ and
$t=d+h$,
\[
    \ell=t+1=d+h+1\leq(d+2)h=qh.
\]
\end{proof}

An incomplete final AB phase

Same mathematical argument

lem:tail · manuscript-main.tex:984–1026

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.envelope_to_dyadic, ModAB.Convergence.Tail, ModAB.Convergence.tail_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.tail_bound

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[An incomplete final AB phase]
\label{lem:tail}
Suppose the first $n$ iterations end either within an AB phase or
immediately after its last AB step, before the following bisection. Let $S$ be the interval length at the
start of this final AB phase. If the unfinished part has length
$\ell_{\mathrm{tail}}$, then
there is an integer $h_{\mathrm{tail}}\geq0$ such that its final width is at
most $S/2^{h_{\mathrm{tail}}}$ and
\begin{equation}
    \ell_{\mathrm{tail}}
    \leq
    qh_{\mathrm{tail}}+(q-1).
    \label{eq:tail_bound}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $t$ be the number of AB steps in the unfinished part.

If the phase is still active and $t\leq d$, take
$h_{\mathrm{tail}}=0$.  Then
$t\leq d=q-2<q-1$.  If the phase is still active and $t\geq d+1$, Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive_envelope} gives
\[
    W(t)\leq\frac{CS}{2^t}\leq\frac{S}{2^{t-d}}.
\]
Thus we may take $h_{\mathrm{tail}}=t-d$, and
$t=d+h_{\mathrm{tail}}\leq qh_{\mathrm{tail}}$.

It remains to consider a final AB step that has just triggered fallback.  If
$t\leq d+1$, again take $h_{\mathrm{tail}}=0$; then
$t\leq d+1=q-1$.  If $t\geq d+2$, the phase was active after step $t-1$, so
\[
    W(t)\leq W(t-1)
    \leq \frac{CS}{2^{t-1}}
    \leq \frac{S}{2^{t-d-1}}.
\]
Now take $h_{\mathrm{tail}}=t-d-1$.  Since
$t=h_{\mathrm{tail}}+d+1$,
\[
    t\leq qh_{\mathrm{tail}}+(q-1).
\]
These cases cover every possible unfinished final part.
\end{proof}

A global bound on the interval length

Same mathematical argument

thm:quantitative · manuscript-main.tex:1028–1078

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.exact_global_width, ModAB.Convergence.width_tendsto_zero_quantitative, ModAB.Convergence.C_at_least_one, ModAB.Convergence.Mode, ModAB.Convergence.History, ModAB.Convergence.CompletedPrefix, ModAB.Convergence.prefix_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.Decomposed, ModAB.Convergence.history_decomposes, ModAB.Convergence.dyadic_compose, ModAB.Convergence.prefix_credits, ModAB.Convergence.history_credits, ModAB.Convergence.credit_floor, ModAB.Convergence.dyadic_antitone, ModAB.Convergence.global_width_bound, ModAB.Convergence.dyadicExponent, ModAB.Convergence.ceil_log_bound, ModAB.Convergence.global_width_bound_log

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{theorem}[A global bound on the interval length]
\label{thm:quantitative}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:global_simple}, after any $n$
completed bracket updates, unless an exact zero was found earlier,
\begin{equation}
    \boxed{
    W_n
    \leq
    W_0\,2^{-\lfloor n/q\rfloor},
    \qquad
    q=\lceil\log_2 C\rceil+2.
    }
    \label{eq:global_width_bound}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Partition the first $n$ iterations into:

\begin{itemize}
    \item bisection steps not already included in a completed phase block;
    \item completed phase blocks from Lemma~\ref{lem:completed_phase};
    \item at most one unfinished final part from Lemma~\ref{lem:tail}.
\end{itemize}

Let $H$ be the sum of the numbers of halvings given by these parts.
A single bisection has length $1$ and gives one halving. Each completed
phase block of length $\ell$ satisfies $\ell\leq qh$, while the
incomplete final part satisfies the bound in Lemma~\ref{lem:tail}.
Adding these inequalities gives
\begin{equation}
    n\leq qH+(q-1).
    \label{eq:amortized_sum}
\end{equation}

Because $H$ is an integer, this implies
\[
    H\geq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{q}\right\rfloor.
\]
Indeed, if $H\leq\lfloor n/q\rfloor-1$, then
\[
    n
    \leq
    q\left(\left\lfloor\frac{n}{q}\right\rfloor-1\right)+(q-1)
    <
    q\left\lfloor\frac{n}{q}\right\rfloor
    \leq n,
\]
which is impossible.  The accumulated halvings give
$W_n\leq W_0/2^H$, and the stated bound follows.
\end{proof}

    C=32,\qquad d=5,\qquad q=7,
\]
and therefore

    \boxed{

Finite termination for an absolute width tolerance

Same mathematical argument

cor:finite_termination · manuscript-main.tex:1110–1141

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Convergence.exact_finite_tolerance, ModAB.Convergence.finite_termination, ModAB.Convergence.toleranceExponent, ModAB.Convergence.tolerance_exponent_sufficient

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{corollary}[Finite termination for an absolute width tolerance]
\label{cor:finite_termination}
Let $\tau>0$, and suppose the method stops as soon as $W_n\leq\tau$.  Define
\[
    K=
    \max\left\{
       0,\,
       \left\lceil\log_2\frac{W_0}{\tau}\right\rceil
    \right\}.
\]
Unless an exact zero is found earlier, $W_{qK}\leq\tau$. Thus the
method terminates after at most
\begin{equation}
    N_{\max}=qK
    \label{eq:finite_bound}
\end{equation}
bracket updates.  For $C=32$, one may take $q=7$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If an exact zero is found earlier, the conclusion is immediate.
Otherwise, by the definition of $K$, $W_0/2^K\leq\tau$. Applying
Theorem~\ref{thm:quantitative} at $n=qK$ gives
\[
    W_{qK}
    \leq
    W_0\,2^{-\lfloor qK/q\rfloor}
    =
    \frac{W_0}{2^K}
    \leq\tau.
\]
\end{proof}

Error bound when the relative rounding model applies

Same mathematical argument

prop:switch_raw_error · manuscript-main.tex:1458–1526

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.gamma, ModAB.unscaled_coefficient, ModAB.unscaled_error_budget, ModAB.Unscaled.eps, ModAB.Unscaled.minNormal, ModAB.Unscaled.NormalOrZero, ModAB.Unscaled.Relative, ModAB.Unscaled.eps_bounds, ModAB.Unscaled.rounded_relative, ModAB.Unscaled.relative_error, ModAB.Unscaled.relative_abs, ModAB.Unscaled.normal_halving_exact, ModAB.Unscaled.factor_two_bound, ModAB.Unscaled.factor_two_gamma, ModAB.Unscaled.factor_three_gamma, ModAB.Unscaled.factor_quotient_gamma, ModAB.Unscaled.Values, ModAB.Unscaled.evaluate, ModAB.Unscaled.arguments, ModAB.Unscaled.NormalRun, ModAB.Unscaled.NoOverflow, ModAB.Unscaled.midpoint_relative, ModAB.Unscaled.quotient_error, ModAB.Unscaled.factor_ranges, ModAB.Unscaled.factor_error, ModAB.Unscaled.distributed_error, ModAB.Unscaled.side_errors, ModAB.Unscaled.unscaled_error, ModAB.Unscaled.comparison_agreement, ModAB.Unscaled.unscaled_comparison

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{proposition}[Error bound when the relative rounding model applies]
\label{prop:switch_raw_error}
Suppose no operation in \eqref{eq:switch_raw_evaluation} overflows,
and every nonzero exact intermediate result lies in the normal range.
With $H=\max(D,|f_3|)$,
\begin{equation}
 \bigl|(\widehat R-\widehat L)-(R-L)\bigr|
 \le 16\epsilon H.
 \label{eq:switch_raw_error}
\end{equation}
The subtraction $\widehat R-\widehat L$ in this bound is an exact
subtraction of the two computed numbers. In particular, the comparison
$\widehat L<\widehat R$ agrees with $L<R$ whenever
$|R-L|>16\epsilon H$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Under the stated assumptions, division of the rounded sum by two is
exact, and
\[
 |\widehat y-y_m|\le\epsilon|y_m|,\qquad
 |\widehat v-v|\le\gamma_3v\le\gamma_3/2.
\]
The quotient estimate accounts for the rounded endpoint sum, endpoint
difference and division. It follows by bounding the relative factor
$(1+\delta_1)(1+\delta_3)/(1+\delta_2)$, where
$|\delta_i|\le\epsilon$. If $y_m=0$, the corresponding quantities are
zero and the estimate remains valid.

The subtraction defining $\widehat r$ and the subsequent square give
\[
 \bigl|\widehat k-(1-\widehat v)^2\bigr|\le\gamma_3.
\]
Indeed, their relative factor contains the rounding error of the
subtraction twice and that of the multiplication once. Since
$0\le v\le1/2$ and $0\le\widehat v<1$, both $1-v$ and
$1-\widehat v$ belong to $[0,1]$. Therefore
\[
 |\widehat k-k|
 \le\gamma_3+2|\widehat v-v|
 \le2\gamma_3.
\]
Also $0\le\widehat k\le1$. For the left-hand side,
\[
 |\widehat L-L|
 \le\epsilon|f_3-\widehat y|+|\widehat y-y_m|
 \le\left(2\epsilon+\frac{\epsilon^2}{2}\right)H.
\]
For the right-hand side, the two rounded products and their rounded
sum differ from $\widehat k(|\widehat y|+|f_3|)$ by at most
$\gamma_2\widehat k(|\widehat y|+|f_3|)$. Hence
\[
 |\widehat R-R|
 \le\left[
   \frac{3+\epsilon}{2}\gamma_2
   +3\gamma_3+\frac{\epsilon}{2}
 \right]H.
\]
Adding the last two estimates gives
\[
 \bigl|(\widehat R-\widehat L)-(R-L)\bigr|
 \le\left[
   \frac{3+\epsilon}{2}\gamma_2+3\gamma_3
   +\frac{5\epsilon+\epsilon^2}{2}
 \right]H
 <16\epsilon H.
\]
This proves the bound and the assertion about the strict comparison.
\end{proof}

A uniform rounding bound for the scaled comparison

Same mathematical argument

thm:switch_scaled_error · manuscript-main.tex:1599–1704

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Binary64.round_interval, ModAB.Binary64.round_abs_le_one, ModAB.Binary64.round_abs_le_two, ModAB.Binary64.abs_le_two_of_unit, ModAB.Binary64.abs_le_two_of_half, ModAB.Binary64.factor_ranges, ModAB.Binary64.opposite_sum_bound, ModAB.Binary64.round_signs, ModAB.Binary64.concrete_gap_error, ModAB.Binary64.concrete_gap_error_mean_value, ModAB.Source.source_error_mean_value, ModAB.Source.binary64_program_error_mean_value, ModAB.Within, ModAB.within_refl, ModAB.within_trans, ModAB.within_add, ModAB.within_sub, ModAB.within_abs, ModAB.within_half, ModAB.within_mono, ModAB.product_error, ModAB.square_error, ModAB.kappa_hasDerivAt, ModAB.kappa_derivative_bounds, ModAB.kappa_lipschitz_ordered, ModAB.kappa_lipschitz, ModAB.ratio_range, ModAB.quotient_error, ModAB.FactorTrace, ModAB.factor_error_from_coefficient, ModAB.factor_error, ModAB.ScaledTrace, ModAB.scaled_errors_from_factor, ModAB.scaled_errors, ModAB.normalized_midpoint_bound

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{theorem}[A uniform rounding bound for the scaled comparison]
\label{thm:switch_scaled_error}
For all finite binary64 inputs with $f_1f_2<0$, the operations in
\eqref{eq:switch_scaled_factor}--\eqref{eq:switch_scaled_evaluation}
produce finite values and have no zero divisor. Under the rounding
assumptions stated above, including gradual underflow,
\begin{equation}
 |\widetilde k-k|\le8\epsilon,\qquad
 |\widehat h-h|\le3\epsilon,\qquad
 |\widehat G-G|\le25\epsilon.
 \label{eq:switch_scaled_error}
\end{equation}
No lower bound on the nonzero input magnitudes is necessary.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First observe that rounding any real number in $[-2,2]$ to binary64
introduces absolute error at most $\epsilon$. The largest spacing
below $2$ is $2^{-52}=2\epsilon$; subnormal spacing is smaller.
This absolute bound therefore remains valid when relative error
estimates fail near zero.

The computed ratio satisfies $0\le\widehat\rho\le1$.
Consequently,
\[
 0\le\widehat a\le1,\qquad
 1\le\widehat b\le2,\qquad
 0\le\widetilde v\le1/2,\qquad
 1/2\le\widetilde r\le1,\qquad
 1/4\le\widetilde k\le1.
\]
Division of $\widehat a$ by two is exact: either $\widehat a=0$,
or $\widehat\rho<1$ and $\widehat a\ge2^{-53}$.
Thus all operations defining the factor are finite and their
divisors are positive.

To bound its error, note that
\[
 \kappa'(\rho)=\frac{1+3\rho}{(1+\rho)^3},\qquad
 0<\kappa'(\rho)\le1\quad(0\le\rho\le1).
\]
Since $|\widehat\rho-\rho|\le\epsilon$, replacing $\rho$ by
$\widehat\rho$ changes $\kappa$ by at most $\epsilon$.
For this fixed $\widehat\rho$, put
$a=1-\widehat\rho$, $b=1+\widehat\rho$ and $v_0=a/(2b)$.
The rounding bounds give
$|\widehat a-a|\le\epsilon$ and
$|\widehat b-b|\le\epsilon$, while $b,\widehat b\ge1$. Hence
\[
 \left|\frac{\widehat a}{2\widehat b}-\frac{a}{2b}\right|
 \le\frac{|\widehat a-a|}{2\widehat b}
   +\frac{a|\widehat b-b|}{2b\widehat b}
 \le\epsilon.
\]
Rounding the division adds at most $\epsilon$, so
$|\widetilde v-v_0|\le2\epsilon$. Rounding the next subtraction
gives $|\widetilde r-(1-v_0)|\le3\epsilon$. Both quantities being
squared lie in $[0,1]$, and therefore
\[
 |\widetilde k-\kappa(\widehat\rho)|
 \le 2(3\epsilon)+\epsilon=7\epsilon.
\]
Combining this with the perturbation of $\rho$ proves the first bound.

Each normalized input satisfies $|p_i|\le1$ and
$|\widehat p_i-p_i|\le\epsilon$. Rounding preserves the opposite
endpoint signs, except that a sufficiently small normalized endpoint
may become zero. In either case,
$|\widehat p_1+\widehat p_2|\le1$ and
$|\widehat h|\le1/2$. The endpoint divisions, the addition and the
halving therefore give
\[
 |\widehat h-h|
 \le\frac{\epsilon+\epsilon+\epsilon}{2}+\epsilon
 \le3\epsilon.
\]
The last $\epsilon$ also covers a subnormal halving.

Write $L_s=|p_3-h|$ and $R_s=k(|h|+|p_3|)$.
The subtraction for the left-hand side gives
\[
 |\widetilde L-L_s|
 \le\epsilon+3\epsilon+\epsilon=5\epsilon.
\]
For the two products on the right-hand side, use
$|p_3|\le1$, $|h|\le1/2$ and $\widetilde k\le1$:
\begin{align*}
 \left|\operatorname{fl}(\widetilde k|\widehat p_3|)
              -k|p_3|\right|
 &\le\epsilon+\epsilon+8\epsilon=10\epsilon,\\
 \left|\operatorname{fl}(\widetilde k|\widehat h|)
              -k|h|\right|
 &\le\epsilon+3\epsilon+4\epsilon=8\epsilon.
\end{align*}
The rounded sum contributes at most another $\epsilon$, giving
$|\widetilde R-R_s|\le19\epsilon$.
All these exact intermediate results have magnitude at most $2$:
the products are bounded by $1$ and $1/2$, respectively, and
both sides of the comparison are at most $3/2$. Thus the absolute
rounding bound applies throughout, and no overflow is possible.
Finally, rounding their difference contributes at most $\epsilon$:
\[
 |\widehat G-G|
 \le19\epsilon+5\epsilon+\epsilon=25\epsilon.
\]
\end{proof}

A sufficient condition for a reliable switching decision

Same mathematical argument

cor:switch_guard · manuscript-main.tex:1706–1727

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Source.guard_literal, ModAB.Source.guard_exact, ModAB.Source.decision_switch_iff, ModAB.Source.decision_no_switch_iff, ModAB.Source.three_input_switch, ModAB.Source.three_input_no_switch, ModAB.Source.executable_margin_guarantees, ModAB.Source.decision, ModAB.guard_positive, ModAB.guard_negative, ModAB.complete_positive, ModAB.complete_negative, ModAB.certified_switch, ModAB.certified_no_switch, ModAB.binary64Epsilon_pos, ModAB.binary64_guard_value, ModAB.scaled_original_switch, ModAB.subnormal_exact_scaled_gap, ModAB.subnormal_margin, ModAB.certified_from_residuals, ModAB.scaled_margin_guarantees

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{corollary}[A sufficient condition for a reliable switching decision]
\label{cor:switch_guard}
With the scaled evaluation above,
\begin{equation}
 \widehat G>32\epsilon=2^{-48}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad L<R.
 \label{eq:switch_guard}
\end{equation}
Likewise, $\widehat G<-32\epsilon$ implies $L>R$.
If neither inequality holds, retaining bisection avoids an uncertified
switch. Every input with $G>57\epsilon$ is accepted by
\eqref{eq:switch_guard}, and every input with $G<-57\epsilon$
is certified as not satisfying the exact switching inequality.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:switch_scaled_error} gives
$G\ge\widehat G-25\epsilon>7\epsilon>0$ in the first case,
and the analogous negative bound in the second. Conversely,
$G>57\epsilon$ implies $\widehat G>32\epsilon$, and similarly
for the negative case.
\end{proof}

Absolute rounding error on the required range

Same mathematical argument

lem:formal_rounding_error · manuscript-main.tex:1877–1907

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Binary64.round_representable, ModAB.Binary64.round_exact, ModAB.Binary64.round_monotone, ModAB.Binary64.power_representable, ModAB.Binary64.round_zero, ModAB.Binary64.round_one, ModAB.Binary64.round_two, ModAB.Binary64.round_half, ModAB.Binary64.round_quarter, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_exact, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_one, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_two, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_half, ModAB.Binary64.round_interval, ModAB.Binary64.round_abs_le_one, ModAB.Binary64.round_abs_le_two, ModAB.Binary64.round_error, ModAB.Binary64.round_finite

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Absolute rounding error on the required range]
\label{lem:formal_rounding_error}
The map $\operatorname{rn}$ is nondecreasing and fixes every number
representable in its binary grid. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 |x|\le2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 |\operatorname{rn}(x)-x|\le\epsilon,
 \qquad |\operatorname{rn}(x)|\le2.
 \label{eq:formal_local_error}
\end{equation}
Consequently, every rounded value in this statement is finite in
binary64.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Monotonicity and preservation of representable values are established
for the rounding map in the underlying formal theory. To obtain the
error bound needed here, suppose first that $0<|x|<2$. Then $m(x)\le1$
and hence $e(x)\le-52$. Since $N$ changes its argument by at most
$1/2$,
\[
 |\operatorname{rn}(x)-x|\le\tfrac12\,2^{e(x)}
 \le2^{-53}=\epsilon.
\]
The remaining values $0$, $2$ and $-2$ are representable and are
unchanged by rounding. Monotonicity, applied between the fixed points
$-2$ and $2$, gives the second inequality in
\eqref{eq:formal_local_error}. Finally, $2<\Omega$, so the finite-range
condition is satisfied. Each of these steps is checked in the Lean
proof; the subnormal range is covered by the same absolute estimate.
\end{proof}

Exact halving in the symmetry factor

Same mathematical argument

lem:formal_factor_halving · manuscript-main.tex:1918–1949

Corresponding Lean declarations

ModAB.Binary64.power_representable, ModAB.Binary64.round_zero, ModAB.Binary64.round_one, ModAB.Binary64.round_two, ModAB.Binary64.round_half, ModAB.Binary64.round_quarter, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_exact, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_one, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_two, ModAB.Binary64.round_neg_half, ModAB.Binary64.epsilon_power, ModAB.Binary64.round_epsilon, ModAB.Binary64.gap_below_one, ModAB.Binary64.exact_half_of_ge_epsilon, ModAB.Binary64.factor_halving_exact

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{lemma}[Exact halving in the symmetry factor]
\label{lem:formal_factor_halving}
If $0\le\rho\le1$, $\widehat\rho=\operatorname{rn}(\rho)$ and
$\widehat a=\operatorname{rn}(1-\widehat\rho)$, then
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{rn}(\widehat a/2)=\widehat a/2.
 \label{eq:formal_factor_halving}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Monotonicity gives $0\le\widehat\rho\le1$. If
$\widehat\rho=1$, then $\widehat a=0$ and the assertion follows.
Otherwise, the spacing immediately below $1$ is $2^{-53}$, so
\[
 1-\widehat\rho\ge2^{-53}=\epsilon.
\]
More explicitly, every representable $x\in[1/2,1)$ is an integer
multiple of $\epsilon$. The strict inequality $x<1=2^{53}\epsilon$
therefore gives $x\le(2^{53}-1)\epsilon$. For $x<1/2$ the same
upper bound is immediate. This proves that there is no representable
number strictly between $1-\epsilon$ and $1$.

Since $\epsilon$ is itself representable, monotonicity now gives
$\widehat a\ge\operatorname{rn}(\epsilon)=\epsilon$.
Also $\widehat a\le1$. Dividing such a representable number by two
lowers its exponent by one and preserves its significand; the result
is at least $2^{-54}$ and remains within the normal range. It is
therefore representable and fixed by rounding. The formal proof
establishes both the spacing assertion and preservation of
representability under this exponent change.
\end{proof}

Verified correspondence and switching decision

Same mathematical argument

Manuscript statement and proof
\begin{theorem}[Verified correspondence and switching decision]
\label{thm:formal_switching}
Let $w_1,w_2,w_3\in\mathcal B$, put $f_i=\nu(w_i)$, and suppose
$f_1f_2<0$. Define $\rho,s,p_i,h,G,L,R$ as in
\eqref{eq:switch_exact_sides}--\eqref{eq:switch_scaled_gap}.
Every intermediate arithmetic result in the evaluation of
$\mathcal E_{64}$ is finite, and every divisor is positive. Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
 \widehat G:=\nu\bigl(\mathcal E_{64}(w_1,w_2,w_3)\bigr)
   =\mathcal E_{\rm rn}(f_1,f_2,f_3),\qquad
 |\widehat G-G|\le25\epsilon.
 \label{eq:formal_complete_error}
\end{equation}
The executable comparisons with the binary64 representations of
$2^{-48}$ and $-2^{-48}$ consequently satisfy
\[
 \widehat G>2^{-48}\ \Longrightarrow\ L<R,
 \qquad
 \widehat G<-2^{-48}\ \Longrightarrow\ R<L.
\]
They select the respective branches whenever $G>57\epsilon$ or
$G<-57\epsilon$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The hypothesis implies that the endpoint magnitudes and their maximum
are positive. It follows that $s>0$, $0<\rho\le1$ and $|p_i|\le1$.
The normalized endpoint values have opposite signs. Their rounded
values retain the weak sign inequalities even if one of them becomes
zero, and hence $|\widehat p_1+\widehat p_2|\le1$.

Monotonicity of $\operatorname{rn}$ gives the intervals for the
computed factor stated in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:switch_scaled_error}, as well as
$|\widehat h|\le1/2$. The exact results to which rounding is applied
all belong to $[-2,2]$. The endpoint maximum and $s$ are positive,
$\widehat b\ge1$, and the remaining divisor is $2$.
These facts are established before using
\eqref{eq:formal_operation_correspondence}.

Apply that relation successively to the arithmetic subexpressions.
For the initial input expressions, equality of real values is the
definition of $f_i$. If equality has already been established for the
operands of an operation, its range bound and, where needed, its
positive divisor allow \eqref{eq:formal_operation_correspondence} to
be applied. The new representation is finite and its value agrees
with the corresponding rounded real expression. Induction over the
expression therefore proves the equality in
\eqref{eq:formal_complete_error} and finiteness of all intermediate
results.

Lemma~\ref{lem:formal_rounding_error} now supplies each local absolute
error bound, and Lemma~\ref{lem:formal_factor_halving} supplies the
exact first halving. The formal real-arithmetic argument then derives
the bounds with constants $8$, $3$, $5$, $19$ and $25$ used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:switch_scaled_error}. These bounds are conclusions
of the proof, not additional assumptions on the calculation. For
example, the change in $\kappa$ is bounded algebraically by
\[
 \kappa(x)-\kappa(y)
 =(x-y)\frac{1+2x+2y+3xy}{(1+x)^2(1+y)^2},
 \qquad x,y\ge0.
\]
The fraction lies in $[0,1]$, which proves
$|\kappa(x)-\kappa(y)|\le|x-y|$ and supplies the estimate used in
the propagation of errors.

Finally, $2^{-48}=32\epsilon$ is exactly representable. The
comparisons on finite binary64 representations are proved to agree
with the strict order of their real values. Applying the error bound
gives the implications and the $57\epsilon$ separation conditions,
as in Corollary~\ref{cor:switch_guard}. The identity
$G=(R-L)/s$, with $s>0$, returns the assertion to the original
unscaled inequality.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{A scaled evaluation valid throughout the finite range.}
Both restrictions can be avoided by evaluating the symmetry factor
and the two sides with separate scales. Let
\begin{equation}
 \rho=\frac{\min(|f_1|,|f_2|)}{\max(|f_1|,|f_2|)},\qquad
 s=\max(|f_1|,|f_2|,|f_3|),\qquad
 p_i=\frac{f_i}{s},\qquad h=\frac{p_1+p_2}{2}.
 \label{eq:switch_scaled_data}
\end{equation}
These definitions use exact real arithmetic. Since the endpoint
values have opposite signs,
\[
 v=\frac{1-\rho}{2(1+\rho)},\qquad
 k=\kappa(\rho):=
 \left(\frac{1+3\rho}{2(1+\rho)}\right)^2.
\]

\end{equation}
Thus $G>0$ is precisely the original switching condition.

\begin{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A sufficient condition for a reliable switching decision]
\label{cor:switch_guard}
With the scaled evaluation above,
\begin{equation}
 \widehat G>32\epsilon=2^{-48}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad L<R.
 \label{eq:switch_guard}
\end{equation}
Likewise, $\widehat G<-32\epsilon$ implies $L>R$.
If neither inequality holds, retaining bisection avoids an uncertified
switch. Every input with $G>57\epsilon$ is accepted by
\eqref{eq:switch_guard}, and every input with $G<-57\epsilon$
is certified as not satisfying the exact switching inequality.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:switch_scaled_error} gives
$G\ge\widehat G-25\epsilon>7\epsilon>0$ in the first case,
and the analogous negative bound in the second. Conversely,
$G>57\epsilon$ implies $\widehat G>32\epsilon$, and similarly

Manuscript passage
\subsection{The rounding map and the two divisions by two}

For $x\ne0$, let $m(x)$ be the unique integer satisfying
\[
 2^{m(x)-1}\le |x|<2^{m(x)},
\]
and put
\begin{equation}
 e(x)=\max\{m(x)-53,-1074\},\qquad
 \operatorname{rn}(x)=2^{e(x)}N\bigl(x2^{-e(x)}\bigr),
 \qquad \operatorname{rn}(0)=0.
 \label{eq:formal_rounding_map}
\end{equation}
Here $N(t)$ is the nearest integer to $t$, with the even integer chosen
when the two distances are equal. The lower bound on $e(x)$ makes the
smallest spacing equal to $\omega=2^{-1074}$. It therefore includes
subnormal numbers and rounding to zero without imposing a relative
error estimate near zero.

The map in \eqref{eq:formal_rounding_map} has no upper exponent bound.
For a value to be a finite binary64 number, it must also satisfy
\begin{equation}
 |x|\le\Omega=(2^{53}-1)2^{971}.
 \label{eq:formal_largest_finite}
\end{equation}
This additional condition is checked when the real-valued map is
related to the finite format. It is not inferred merely from the
precision of the significand.

\begin{lemma}[Absolute rounding error on the required range]
\label{lem:formal_rounding_error}
The map $\operatorname{rn}$ is nondecreasing and fixes every number
representable in its binary grid. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 |x|\le2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 |\operatorname{rn}(x)-x|\le\epsilon,
 \qquad |\operatorname{rn}(x)|\le2.
 \label{eq:formal_local_error}
\end{equation}
Consequently, every rounded value in this statement is finite in
binary64.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Monotonicity and preservation of representable values are established
for the rounding map in the underlying formal theory. To obtain the

F3 · Primitive IEEE operation correspondence

Same mathematical argument

thm:formal_switching · manuscript-main.tex:1951–1974

Finiteness and real interpretation of add, subtract, multiply and divide; exact abs/min/max.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.IEEE64.one, ModAB.IEEE64.two, ModAB.IEEE64.one_valid, ModAB.IEEE64.one_value, ModAB.IEEE64.two_valid, ModAB.IEEE64.two_value, ModAB.IEEE64.abs_value, ModAB.IEEE64.min_valid, ModAB.IEEE64.max_valid, ModAB.IEEE64.Op, ModAB.IEEE64.Op.exact, ModAB.IEEE64.Op.bits, ModAB.IEEE64.Expr, ModAB.IEEE64.Expr.real, ModAB.IEEE64.Expr.bits, ModAB.IEEE64.Expr.Safe, ModAB.IEEE64.Word, ModAB.IEEE64.value, ModAB.IEEE64.valid, ModAB.IEEE64.descriptor_round, ModAB.IEEE64.descriptor_max, ModAB.IEEE64.two_le_max, ModAB.IEEE64.add_refines, ModAB.IEEE64.sub_refines, ModAB.IEEE64.mul_refines, ModAB.IEEE64.div_refines

Manuscript passage
\subsection{The finite-format statement}

Let $\mathcal B$ denote the set of finite binary64 representations and
let $\nu:\mathcal B\to\mathbb R$ assign to each representation its
exact real value. The two representations of zero have the same value
under $\nu$. Arithmetic on representations is defined with IEEE
rounding to nearest, ties to even, including gradual underflow.

For finite operands $u,w$ and an arithmetic operation $\circ$, the
formal library supplies the relation between the executable operation
and its exact real result. In the range used here, this gives
\begin{equation}
 \nu\bigl(\operatorname{op}_{\circ}(u,w)\bigr)
   =\operatorname{rn}\bigl(\nu(u)\circ\nu(w)\bigr)
 \quad\text{if}\quad
 |\nu(u)\circ\nu(w)|\le2,
 \label{eq:formal_operation_correspondence}
\end{equation}
where division additionally requires $\nu(w)\ne0$.
The result is proved finite as part of the assertion, so $\nu$ is
defined on its left-hand side. This is used separately for addition,
subtraction, multiplication and division. Absolute value and the
selection of a minimum or maximum preserve the corresponding real
values exactly.

\subsection{Correspondence with the C\# expression and reproducibility}

The accompanying C\# implementation of the scaled comparison uses
separate addition, subtraction, multiplication and division calls.
A small source translator records their arithmetic expressions in
Lean, preserving the order and nesting of all 17 operations. Equality
with the rounded expression used in the proof is then checked in
Lean. In particular, the two products in the right-hand side are
rounded separately before their sum is formed. The decimal constant
used for the comparison is also checked to equal $2^{-48}$ exactly.
The real-value correspondence is sufficient for the switching
criterion since the distinction between positive and negative zero cannot
change the comparison, and none of the divisors can be zero.

Q3 · Derivative and endpoint bounds of Q

I1 · Strict interiority by convex combination

Same mathematical argument

lem:interiority · manuscript-main.tex:454–475

Both endpoint sign orientations are proved explicitly.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.secant_convex, ModAB.Convergence.secant_interior, ModAB.Convergence.proposal_interior

Manuscript passage
\begin{lemma}[Interiority of the AB approximation]
\label{lem:interiority}
Let $a<b$ and $uv<0$.  Then
\[
    x=\frac{av-bu}{v-u}
\]
belongs to $(a,b)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume without loss of generality that $u<0<v$.  Then
\[
    x
    =
    \frac{av+b|u|}{v+|u|}
    =
    \lambda a+(1-\lambda)b,
    \qquad
    \lambda=\frac{v}{v+|u|}\in(0,1).
\]
Thus $x$ is a strict convex combination of $a$ and $b$ and belongs to
$(a,b)$. The case $v<0<u$ follows in the same way.

I2 · Preserve signs of modified ordinates

Same mathematical argument

lem:working_signs · manuscript-main.tex:478–489

Multiply the retained ordinate by a positive correction factor; insert the true value at the new endpoint.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.SameSign, ModAB.Convergence.positive_correction, ModAB.Convergence.inserted_sign, ModAB.Convergence.working_opposite, ModAB.Convergence.proposal_interior

Manuscript passage
\begin{lemma}[Preservation of the signs of the modified ordinates]
\label{lem:working_signs}
Suppose \eqref{eq:working_signs} holds.  If the retained modified ordinate is
multiplied by a finite factor $\theta>0$ and the newly inserted endpoint
receives the actual function value at the new point, then
\eqref{eq:working_signs} holds at the next nonterminating AB step.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Multiplication by a positive factor preserves the sign of the retained
ordinate. At the new endpoint, the modified ordinate equals the actual value of the function. Since the new interval has endpoint values of opposite
signs, the modified ordinates also have opposite signs.

I3 · Sign update, nested brackets, IVT, and halving

Same mathematical argument

prop:bracket · manuscript-main.tex:492–522

Updated uses the actual conditional; exact_invariants and exact_zeros propagate it along an exact run.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.sign_update, ModAB.Convergence.interval_update, ModAB.Convergence.bisection_width, ModAB.Convergence.bracket_contains_zero, ModAB.Convergence.Updated, ModAB.Convergence.updated_invariants, ModAB.Convergence.exact_invariants, ModAB.Convergence.exact_zeros

Manuscript passage
\begin{proposition}[Nested intervals containing a zero]
\label{prop:bracket}
At every nonterminating iteration,
\begin{equation}
    f(a_n)f(b_n)<0,
    \qquad
    I_{n+1}\subseteq I_n.
    \label{eq:nested_brackets}
\end{equation}
Consequently, every $I_n$ contains at least one zero of $f$, and the widths
\begin{equation}
    W_n=b_n-a_n
    \label{eq:width}
\end{equation}
are nonincreasing.  A bisection step gives
\begin{equation}
    W_{n+1}=\frac{1}{2}W_n.
    \label{eq:bisection_halving}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The sign condition holds initially by Assumption~1. The midpoint belongs
to the current interval, as does the AB approximation by
Lemma~\ref{lem:interiority}. Unless this new point is an exact zero,
precisely one of the two resulting subintervals has endpoint values of
opposite signs. The method selects this subinterval, which contains a
zero by continuity and the intermediate value theorem. This proves the
nesting property and the monotonicity of the interval lengths. A
bisection step gives a subinterval of half the preceding length.
\end{proof}

Same mathematical argument

\begin{lemma}[Decrease of the smaller endpoint residual]
\label{lem:residual_progress}
Let $R^-=\min(|f(a)|,|f(b)|)>0$ before a nonterminating step within
the interval, and let
$R^+$ be the corresponding minimum after the sign-preserving update.
If the new residual satisfies $|f(x)|<\eta R^-$ with $0<\eta<1$, then
\begin{equation}
 R^+=|f(x)|<\eta R^-.
 \label{eq:residual_contraction}
\end{equation}
For $k$ consecutive such steps, the final minimum is less than
$\eta^k$ times its initial value.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The retained endpoint has residual magnitude at least $R^-$. The newly
inserted endpoint has residual magnitude below $\eta R^-<R^-$. It therefore
becomes the endpoint with the smaller residual magnitude, which proves
\eqref{eq:residual_contraction}. Repeated substitution proves the last claim.
\end{proof}

The minimum must be taken before replacing the endpoint. After the
replacement it would be no greater than $|f(x)|$, making the strict

ModAB.Convergence.Step, ModAB.Convergence.Run, ModAB.Convergence.step_history, ModAB.Convergence.step_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.history_of_run, ModAB.Convergence.run_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.dyadic_tendsto, ModAB.Convergence.active_step, ModAB.Convergence.active_history, ModAB.Convergence.continuing_suffix, ModAB.Convergence.finite_bisections, ModAB.Convergence.width_tendsto_zero, ModAB.Convergence.ExactRun, ModAB.Convergence.ExactRun.widthRun, ModAB.Convergence.global_convergence

Manuscript passage
\emph{Case 2: finitely many bisection steps.}
After the last bisection, all subsequent steps belong to a single AB
phase. Indeed, ending that phase would require another bisection, contrary
to the choice of the last one.

Let $W_c$ be the width at the start of that phase and let $W(t)$ be the width
after $t$ AB steps.  Because the phase is still active, Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive_envelope} gives
\[
    W(t)\leq \frac{C W_c}{2^t}.
\]
The right-hand side tends to zero, and hence so does $W(t)$.

G3 · A singleton intersection of nested intervals

Same mathematical argument

thm:global_simple · manuscript-main.tex:860–866

Existence comes from the standard nested compact-set theorem; shrinking diameter proves uniqueness.

Manuscript passage
Thus $W_n\to0$ in either case.  By
Proposition~\ref{prop:bracket}, the closed intervals $I_n$ are nonempty,
nested, and contain a zero.  A standard nested-interval argument now shows that
their intersection consists of one point:
\[
    \bigcap_{n=0}^{\infty} I_n=\{x^\ast\}.
\]

G4 · The common point is a zero

Same mathematical argument

Manuscript passage
To verify that this point is a zero, choose any zero $z_n\in I_n$.  Both
$z_n$ and $x^\ast$ lie in the same interval, so
\[
    |z_n-x^\ast|\leq W_n\longrightarrow0.
\]
Thus $z_n\to x^\ast$.  Since $f(z_n)=0$ and $f$ is continuous,
$f(x^\ast)=0$.

G5 · Additional quantitative route

Additional result

thm:global_simple · manuscript-main.tex:827–840

Manuscript passage
\begin{theorem}[Global convergence]
\label{thm:global_simple}
Under Assumption~1, with the actual function values and modified ordinates
kept separate and the adaptive rule of Section~\ref{sec:method}, the
exact-arithmetic iteration, continued without an iteration limit, either
terminates at an exact zero or satisfies
\[
    W_n\longrightarrow0.
\]
In the latter case, the nested brackets shrink to a single point $x^\ast$, and that point satisfies
$f(x^\ast)=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

P1 · Completed phase: short and long cases

Same mathematical argument

lem:completed_phase · manuscript-main.tex:911–981

For t≤d+1 assign h=1; otherwise h=t−d. Prove width shrinkage and t+1≤qh.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.C_at_least_one, ModAB.Convergence.envelope_to_dyadic, ModAB.Convergence.phaseCredit, ModAB.Convergence.completed_phase

Manuscript passage
\begin{lemma}[Guaranteed shrinkage of a completed AB phase]
\label{lem:completed_phase}
Let a completed phase block start with width $S$.  At its end the width is at
most $S/2^h$, where
\begin{equation}
    h=
    \begin{cases}
       1, & t\leq d+1,\\
       t-d, & t\geq d+2.
    \end{cases}
    \label{eq:phase_credit}
\end{equation}
In both cases,
\begin{equation}
    \ell\leq qh.
    \label{eq:length_credit}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We consider the two ranges of $t$ separately.

If $t\leq d+1$, the AB steps do not increase the interval length, and the
following bisection halves it. Thus $h=1$, and
\[
    \ell=t+1\leq d+2=q=qh.
\]

Now suppose that $t\geq d+2$.  The phase was still active after step $t-1$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive_envelope} therefore gives
\[
    W(t-1)
    \leq
    \frac{CS}{2^{t-1}}
    \leq
    \frac{S}{2^{t-d-1}},
\]
where the last inequality uses $C\leq2^d$.  The $t$th AB step cannot increase
the width, and the following bisection divides it by two.  Hence
\[
    W_{\mathrm{end}}
    \leq
    \frac{S}{2^{t-d}}.
\]
The block therefore guarantees $h=t-d$ halvings.  Since $h\geq2$ and
$t=d+h$,
\[
    \ell=t+1=d+h+1\leq(d+2)h=qh.
\]
\end{proof}

Table~\ref{tab:phase_accounting} summarizes the two estimates. For a short
AB phase, the following bisection alone gives the required reduction.
For a long phase, the geometric bound already implies a stronger
reduction before that bisection.

\begin{table}[H]
\caption{Shrinkage guaranteed by a completed phase block.}
\label{tab:phase_accounting}
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
AB steps $t$ & Guaranteed halvings $h$ & Iteration cost \\ \midrule
$t\leq d+1$ & $1$ & $t+1\leq d+2=q$\\
$t\geq d+2$ & $t-d$ & $t+1\leq q(t-d)$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The first $n$ iterations may end within an AB phase or just after its
last AB step, before the following bisection. We therefore also need an

P2 · Incomplete tail: four cases

Same mathematical argument

lem:tail · manuscript-main.tex:984–1024

Active or stopped, each short or long, gives width≤S/2ʰ and cost≤qh+q−1.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.envelope_to_dyadic, ModAB.Convergence.Tail, ModAB.Convergence.tail_nonnegative, ModAB.Convergence.tail_bound

Manuscript passage
\begin{lemma}[An incomplete final AB phase]
\label{lem:tail}
Suppose the first $n$ iterations end either within an AB phase or
immediately after its last AB step, before the following bisection. Let $S$ be the interval length at the
start of this final AB phase. If the unfinished part has length
$\ell_{\mathrm{tail}}$, then
there is an integer $h_{\mathrm{tail}}\geq0$ such that its final width is at
most $S/2^{h_{\mathrm{tail}}}$ and
\begin{equation}
    \ell_{\mathrm{tail}}
    \leq
    qh_{\mathrm{tail}}+(q-1).
    \label{eq:tail_bound}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $t$ be the number of AB steps in the unfinished part.

If the phase is still active and $t\leq d$, take
$h_{\mathrm{tail}}=0$.  Then
$t\leq d=q-2<q-1$.  If the phase is still active and $t\geq d+1$, Lemma~\ref{lem:adaptive_envelope} gives
\[
    W(t)\leq\frac{CS}{2^t}\leq\frac{S}{2^{t-d}}.
\]
Thus we may take $h_{\mathrm{tail}}=t-d$, and
$t=d+h_{\mathrm{tail}}\leq qh_{\mathrm{tail}}$.

It remains to consider a final AB step that has just triggered fallback.  If
$t\leq d+1$, again take $h_{\mathrm{tail}}=0$; then
$t\leq d+1=q-1$.  If $t\geq d+2$, the phase was active after step $t-1$, so
\[
    W(t)\leq W(t-1)
    \leq \frac{CS}{2^{t-1}}
    \leq \frac{S}{2^{t-d-1}}.
\]
Now take $h_{\mathrm{tail}}=t-d-1$.  Since
$t=h_{\mathrm{tail}}+d+1$,
\[
    t\leq qh_{\mathrm{tail}}+(q-1).
\]

\begin{proof}
Partition the first $n$ iterations into:

P4 · Sum integer credits

Same mathematical argument

thm:quantitative · manuscript-main.tex:1053–1061

Compose the dyadic factors and sum costs to obtain Wₙ≤W₀/2ᴴ and n≤qH+q−1.

Lean declarations for this step

Manuscript passage
Let $H$ be the sum of the numbers of halvings given by these parts.
A single bisection has length $1$ and gives one halving. Each completed
phase block of length $\ell$ satisfies $\ell\leq qh$, while the
incomplete final part satisfies the bound in Lemma~\ref{lem:tail}.

\begin{equation}
    n\leq qH+(q-1).
    \label{eq:amortized_sum}
\end{equation}

P5 · Take the integer floor

Same mathematical argument

thm:quantitative · manuscript-main.tex:1063–1078

Derive H≥floor(n/q); identify d=ceil(log₂ C) and q=d+2.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.credit_floor, ModAB.Convergence.dyadic_antitone, ModAB.Convergence.global_width_bound, ModAB.Convergence.dyadicExponent, ModAB.Convergence.ceil_log_bound, ModAB.Convergence.global_width_bound_log, ModAB.Convergence.exact_global_width

Manuscript passage
Because $H$ is an integer, this implies
\[
    H\geq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{q}\right\rfloor.
\]
Indeed, if $H\leq\lfloor n/q\rfloor-1$, then
\[
    n
    \leq
    q\left(\left\lfloor\frac{n}{q}\right\rfloor-1\right)+(q-1)
    <
    q\left\lfloor\frac{n}{q}\right\rfloor
    \leq n,
\]
which is impossible.  The accumulated halvings give
$W_n\leq W_0/2^H$, and the stated bound follows.
\end{proof}

P7 · Finite width tolerance

Same mathematical argument

cor:finite_termination · manuscript-main.tex:1110–1144

K=max(0,ceil(log₂(W₀/τ))); after qK iterations the continued width is at most τ.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.finite_termination, ModAB.Convergence.tolerance_exponent_sufficient, ModAB.Convergence.toleranceExponent, ModAB.Convergence.exact_finite_tolerance

Manuscript passage
\begin{corollary}[Finite termination for an absolute width tolerance]
\label{cor:finite_termination}
Let $\tau>0$, and suppose the method stops as soon as $W_n\leq\tau$.  Define
\[
    K=
    \max\left\{
       0,\,
       \left\lceil\log_2\frac{W_0}{\tau}\right\rceil
    \right\}.
\]
Unless an exact zero is found earlier, $W_{qK}\leq\tau$. Thus the
method terminates after at most
\begin{equation}
    N_{\max}=qK
    \label{eq:finite_bound}
\end{equation}
bracket updates.  For $C=32$, one may take $q=7$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If an exact zero is found earlier, the conclusion is immediate.
Otherwise, by the definition of $K$, $W_0/2^K\leq\tau$. Applying
Theorem~\ref{thm:quantitative} at $n=qK$ gives
\[
    W_{qK}
    \leq
    W_0\,2^{-\lfloor qK/q\rfloor}
    =
    \frac{W_0}{2^K}
    \leq\tau.
\]
\end{proof}

\subsection{An error bound at termination}

P8 · Distance to a zero

Same mathematical argument

eq:distance_zero_set · manuscript-main.tex:1145–1161

For any selected point in a bracket of width≤τ, exhibit a zero no farther than τ.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Convergence.interval_distance, ModAB.Convergence.distance_to_zero

Manuscript passage
Let
\[
    Z=\{z\in[a_0,b_0]:f(z)=0\}
\]
be the set of all zeros in the initial bracket.  If the algorithm returns a
point $x_n\in I_n$ after a width test $W_n\leq\tau$, then
\begin{equation}
    \operatorname{dist}(x_n,Z)\leq\tau.
    \label{eq:distance_zero_set}
\end{equation}
Here $\operatorname{dist}(x_n,Z)$ means the distance from $x_n$ to the nearest
zero in $Z$.  The estimate is immediate: the current bracket contains at least
one zero $z$, and both $x_n$ and $z$ lie in an interval of width at most
$\tau$.

Using the whole zero set rather than a preselected zero is standard in
variational analysis \cite{RockafellarWets1998,DontchevRockafellar2014}.  It is

U6 · Strict comparison agreement

Same mathematical argument

prop:switch_raw_error · manuscript-main.tex:1458–1471

A gap with magnitude greater than 16εH has the same strict comparison sign after the two sides are computed.

Lean declarations for this step

Manuscript passage
\begin{proposition}[Error bound when the relative rounding model applies]
\label{prop:switch_raw_error}
Suppose no operation in \eqref{eq:switch_raw_evaluation} overflows,
and every nonzero exact intermediate result lies in the normal range.
With $H=\max(D,|f_3|)$,
\begin{equation}
 \bigl|(\widehat R-\widehat L)-(R-L)\bigr|
 \le 16\epsilon H.
 \label{eq:switch_raw_error}
\end{equation}
The subtraction $\widehat R-\widehat L$ in this bound is an exact
subtraction of the two computed numbers. In particular, the comparison
$\widehat L<\widehat R$ agrees with $L<R$ whenever
$|R-L|>16\epsilon H$.

\begin{proposition}[A one-sided quadratic AB phase]
\label{prop:one_sided}
For the continuously differentiable function
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=\begin{cases}x,&x\le0,\\x+x^2,&x>0,\end{cases}
 \label{eq:one_sided_function}
\end{equation}
an AB phase starting from $[-1,1]$ with the actual endpoint values as its
initial ordinates has a fixed
right endpoint. If interpolation is continued without a width fallback,
the left-endpoint error converges quadratically to zero, whereas the bracket
width tends to one.
\end{proposition}

UC1 · Function, regularity, and unique zero

Same mathematical argument

prop:uncapped_counterexample · manuscript-main.tex:617–639

Proves the piecewise formula, C¹ gluing at zero, and f(x)=0 iff x=0.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counterFunction_formula, ModAB.Examples.counterFunction_C1, ModAB.Examples.counterFunction_zero_iff

Manuscript passage
\begin{proposition}[Residual contraction without width convergence]
\label{prop:uncapped_counterexample}
Consider the usual AB correction, the switching test
\eqref{eq:switch_test}, and the rule with $B=2$, $\eta=1/2$, but with
no bound on the number of residual exceptions. For
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=\begin{cases}-x^2,&x<0,\\7x^2,&x\ge0,\end{cases}
 \qquad [a_0,b_0]=[-3,1],
 \label{eq:cap_counterexample}
\end{equation}
the exact-arithmetic process enters an AB phase after its first bisection
and never leaves it. The left endpoints converge to the unique
zero, while the right endpoint remains $1$ and the width tends to $1$.
From the third AB step onward, the ratio of the new residual magnitude to
the smaller preceding endpoint residual magnitude is at most $0.49<1/2$.
The function belongs to $C^1(\mathbb R)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The only zero is $0$. On the two open half-lines the derivatives are $-2x$
and $14x$, respectively. At zero,
$f'(0)=\lim_{h\to0}f(h)/h=0$, and both one-sided derivatives tend to zero.
This proves the stated regularity.

UC3 · Secant error and first two updates

Same mathematical argument

eq:cap_secant_error · manuscript-main.tex:646–661

Derives the secant error from the original coordinates and checks e₁=3/4, e₂=75/121.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.left_secant_identity, ModAB.Examples.counter_first_steps, ModAB.Examples.counter_trial_identity

Manuscript passage
Let $j$ count AB steps after this entry. Write the left endpoint as $-e_j$
and the retained right modified ordinate as $v_j$, with $e_0=1$ and $v_0=7$.
The actual function value at the left endpoint is $-e_j^2$. If the next trial point remains to the
left of the zero, the secant formula gives
\begin{equation}
 e_j=\frac{e_{j-1}v_{j-1}-e_{j-1}^2}
              {v_{j-1}+e_{j-1}^2}.
 \label{eq:cap_secant_error}
\end{equation}
There is no retained-ordinate rescaling on the first AB step. Hence
\[
 e_1=\frac{7-1}{7+1}=\frac34,\qquad v_1=7,
 \qquad e_2=\frac{7e_1-e_1^2}{7+e_1^2}=\frac{75}{121}.
\]
Both approximations lie strictly to the left of zero and are closer to
it than the preceding left endpoint.

UC4 · Correction identity and retained ordinate

Same mathematical argument

eq:cap_working_ordinate · manuscript-main.tex:663–677

Proves the balance (e−t)v=e²(1+t), then the corrected ordinate (1+t)(e+t), and uses it in the actual state transition.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counter_correction_identity, ModAB.Examples.counter_state_step

Manuscript passage
For two successive updates of that endpoint, the AB multiplier is
$1-e_j^2/e_{j-1}^2$, which is positive whenever $0<e_j<e_{j-1}$.
Rearranging \eqref{eq:cap_secant_error} gives
\[
 (e_{j-1}-e_j)v_{j-1}=e_{j-1}^2(1+e_j).
\]
Thus, for $j\ge2$ while these inequalities hold,
\begin{align}
 v_j
 &=v_{j-1}\left(1-\frac{e_j^2}{e_{j-1}^2}\right)\notag\\
 &=\frac{e_{j-1}^2(1+e_j)}{e_{j-1}-e_j}
   \frac{(e_{j-1}-e_j)(e_{j-1}+e_j)}{e_{j-1}^2}\notag\\
 &=(1+e_j)(e_{j-1}+e_j).
 \label{eq:cap_working_ordinate}
\end{align}

UC6 · Ratio recurrence and partial derivatives

To prove that all subsequent approximations have the same properties, put $\rho_j=e_j/e_{j-1}$.
Using \eqref{eq:cap_working_ordinate} in the next secant, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \frac{e_{j+1}}{e_j}
 &=\frac{v_j-e_j}{v_j+e_j^2}\\
 &=\frac{(1+e_j)e_{j-1}+e_j^2}
         {(1+e_j)e_{j-1}+e_j+2e_j^2}\\
 &=\frac{1+e_j(1+\rho_j)}
         {1+\rho_j+e_j(1+2\rho_j)}.
\end{align*}
The last equality divides numerator and denominator by $e_{j-1}>0$.
Define
\[
 \Phi(e,\rho)=\frac{1+e(1+\rho)}{1+\rho+e(1+2\rho)}.
\]
For $e\ge0$ and $\rho>0$ its denominator is positive, and direct
differentiation gives
\[
 \frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\rho}
 =-\frac{(1+e)^2}{[1+\rho+e(1+2\rho)]^2}<0,\qquad
 \frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial e}
 =\frac{\rho^2}{[1+\rho+e(1+2\rho)]^2}>0.

UC7 · Invariant rectangle

Same mathematical argument

eq:cap_invariant_region · manuscript-main.tex:708–717

The same corner bounds 10/17 and 16/23 imply 1/2≤r≤7/10; induction preserves the error and ratio bounds.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.phi_region, ModAB.Examples.counter_invariant

Manuscript passage
On $0\le e\le2/5$, $1/2\le\rho\le7/10$, monotonicity therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{10}{17}=\Phi(0,7/10)
 \le\Phi(e,\rho)\le\Phi(2/5,1/2)=\frac{16}{23}<\frac7{10}.
 \label{eq:cap_invariant_region}
\end{equation}
Both bounds belong to $[1/2,7/10]$. Since the required inequalities hold
for $j=3$, induction gives
\[
 0<e_{j+1}\le\tfrac7{10}e_j,\qquad

UC8 · Positive shrinking errors, fixed right endpoint

Same mathematical argument

prop:uncapped_counterexample · manuscript-main.tex:714–723

Inductively connects the recurrence to every actual AB state, derives eₙ₊₁≤0.7eₙ, proves eₙ→0, and verifies every sign update.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counter_orbit, ModAB.Examples.counter_error_shrinks, ModAB.Examples.counterError_tendsto, ModAB.Examples.counter_full_actual_update, ModAB.Examples.counter_full_ordinate_positive

Manuscript passage
Both bounds belong to $[1/2,7/10]$. Since the required inequalities hold
for $j=3$, induction gives
\[
 0<e_{j+1}\le\tfrac7{10}e_j,\qquad
 \rho_{j+1}\in[1/2,7/10].
\]
These inequalities simultaneously prove that the next trial point remains
strictly left of zero, that the AB multiplier stays positive, and that
the hypotheses are preserved. Thus $e_j\to0$ and the right endpoint
is never replaced.

UC9 · True residual ratio at most 0.49

Same mathematical argument

eq:cap_residual_ratio · manuscript-main.tex:725–732

Identifies the pre-update minimum and uses the squared error ratio, obtaining the strict residual exception for η=1/2.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counter_residual_ratio, ModAB.Examples.counter_residual_exception

Manuscript passage
The actual value of the function at the right endpoint is $7$ and the left residual magnitude is
$e_j^2\le1$. Before AB step $j$ the minimum residual magnitude is therefore
$e_{j-1}^2$. For every $j\ge3$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{|f(-e_j)|}{\min\{|f(-e_{j-1})|,|f(1)|\}}
 =\rho_j^2\le(7/10)^2=0.49<\tfrac12.
 \label{eq:cap_residual_ratio}
\end{equation}

UC10 · Width-test failure and indefinite AB continuation

Same mathematical argument

prop:uncapped_counterexample · manuscript-main.tex:734–740

First two width tests pass; all subsequent width tests fail while the residual exception passes. The uncapped rule continues forever, with widths tending to one.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counter_width_test_fails, ModAB.Examples.uncappedContinues, ModAB.Examples.counter_uncapped_continues, ModAB.Examples.counter_full_width_limit, ModAB.Examples.uncapped_counterexample

Manuscript passage
At the beginning of the phase the width is $S=2$, so the threshold at AB step $j$ is
$T_j=4/2^{j-1}$. The first two interval lengths satisfy the respective bounds
$4$ and $2$. For $j\ge3$, however, $T_j\le1$ while
$W_j^{\rm AB}=1+e_j>1$. The width condition is therefore violated at every subsequent step,
whereas \eqref{eq:cap_residual_ratio} permits a residual exception at each
of these steps. Without a bound on their number, the AB phase continues
indefinitely and the interval length tends to $1$.

UC11 · Violation of the global width bound

Same mathematical argument

eq:cap_global_violation · manuscript-main.tex:743–750

Preserves total-iteration indexing: iteration 14 is one bisection plus 13 AB steps. The claimed bound equals one but the width exceeds one.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counter_global_bound_violation

Manuscript passage
The same example shows that the earlier global estimate cannot be
retained after removing the bound on consecutive exceptions. If $N$ counts all bracket updates,
including the initial bisection, then $W_0=4$ and
\begin{equation}
 W_{14}=1+e_{13}>1
       =W_0\,2^{-\lfloor14/7\rfloor}.
 \label{eq:cap_global_violation}
\end{equation}

UC12 · Failure of width and mixed-tolerance stopping

Same mathematical argument

prop:uncapped_counterexample · manuscript-main.tex:751–758

Proves widths exceed every τ≤1 and eventually exceed εa+εr|x| for εa<1 and any fixed finite εr.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Examples.counter_no_width_termination, ModAB.Examples.counter_mixed_tolerance_fails

Manuscript passage
The approximations themselves still converge to the zero. What fails is
convergence of the whole bracket and, consequently, width-based termination
at a tolerance below the limiting width. For the mixed tolerance used here,
any fixed $\varepsilon_{\rm abs}<1$ and finite $\varepsilon_{\rm rel}$
leave the width test unsatisfied for all sufficiently late steps, since the
candidate tends to zero. In particular, the tolerances of the numerical
experiment in Section~\ref{subsec:native_cap} cannot terminate this
exact-arithmetic process by width. It never encounters an exact zero.

D1 · Mean-value point and derivative lower bound

Same mathematical argument

eq:residual_bound · manuscript-main.tex:1165–1176

Continuity and interior differentiability yield c; the bound on |f′(c)| bounds the residual from below.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Residual.mean_value_lower_bound

Manuscript passage
If, however, the bracket contains a unique zero $x^\ast$, $f$ is
differentiable, and
\[
    |f'(x)|\geq m>0
\]
throughout the bracket, the mean value theorem gives
\begin{equation}
    |x_n-x^\ast|
    \leq
    \frac{|f(x_n)|}{m}.
    \label{eq:residual_bound}
\end{equation}

Same mathematical argument

Applies the bound between x and a zero z, treats either order and equality, and divides by m>0. The proof does not require uniqueness.

Lean declarations for this step

ModAB.Residual.residual_distance

Manuscript passage
    \frac{|f(x_n)|}{m}.
    \label{eq:residual_bound}
\end{equation}

ModAB.Binary64.round_error

Same mathematical argument

theorem · ModAB/Binary64.lean:95–119

lem:formal_rounding_error

Separates 0 and ±2; inside the range proves magnitude≤1, exponent≤−52, ulp≤2⁻⁵², and half-ulp≤ε.

Steps: R2

Lean declaration and proof
theorem round_error {x : ℝ} (hx : |x| ≤ 2) : Within (round x) x binary64Epsilon := by
  by_cases h0 : x = 0
  · subst x; simpa [Within] using le_of_lt binary64Epsilon_pos
  by_cases hp : x = 2
  · subst x; simpa [Within] using le_of_lt binary64Epsilon_pos
  by_cases hn : x = -2
  · subst x; simpa [Within] using le_of_lt binary64Epsilon_pos
  have hlt : |x| < 2 := by
    rcases le_total 0 x with h | h
    · rw [abs_of_nonneg h] at hx ⊢; exact lt_of_le_of_ne hx hp
    · rw [abs_of_nonpos h] at hx ⊢; exact lt_of_le_of_ne hx (by intro he; apply hn; linarith)
  have hmag : magnitude binaryRadix x ≤ 1 :=
    magnitude_le_of_abs_lt_bpow binaryRadix x 1 h0 (by simpa [pow_value] using hlt)
  have hexp : cexp binaryRadix exponent x ≤ -52 := by
    dsimp [cexp, exponent, fltExp]
    exact max_le (by omega) (by norm_num)
  have hpw := (bpow_le_bpow_iff binaryRadix _ _).mpr hexp
  have hu : ulp binaryRadix exponent x ≤ (2:ℝ)^(-52:ℤ) := by
    rw [ulp.of_ne_zero _ _ _ h0]
    simpa [pow_value] using hpw
  have he := error_bound_ulp (β := binaryRadix) (fexp := exponent) nearestEven x
  change |round x-x| ≤ _ at he
  apply le_trans he
  have h := div_le_div_of_nonneg_right hu (by norm_num : (0:ℝ) ≤ 2)
  convert h using 1 <;> norm_num [binary64Epsilon]
